It’s not constant agony, but it wears me down, and it can make it hard to focus on anything else until the worry loosens its grip a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, it feels a little like that in the moment, but I don’t know that I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more… I’m trying to get rid of the uncertainty, I think.\n\nLike, if I go back over something, it’s usually because I have this nagging feeling that I may have missed something or gotten something wrong, and I want to settle that feeling. It’s more about trying to quiet the doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the worries that lead you to recheck things are related to something potentially disastrous happening if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always “disastrous” in a dramatic way, like I’m picturing one single terrible event every time, but it does often feel like if I don’t check, something could start going wrong and then keep getting worse.\n\nOver this past year especially, it’s spread out a lot. At first I think most of my worry was centered on my mother’s safety, like whether something might happen and I wouldn’t catch it or I hadn’t done enough. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, things at work, decisions involving staff, deadlines, the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they’re on the verge of unraveling if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nSo when I recheck, it’s usually because my mind is saying, “If you missed this, there could be consequences.” Maybe not always a catastrophe, but something bad enough that I feel responsible for preventing it. And I know sometimes the level of worry doesn’t match the situation, but in the moment it can feel like I’m keeping something from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are mainly because you're afraid of making a mistake if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not always just, “Oh no, I made a typo,” or something simple like that, but it’s that feeling that if I don’t catch something, I’ll be responsible for what happens next.\n\nAnd over the past year it’s gotten… broader, I guess. It used to be much more focused on my mother’s safety, like worrying I hadn’t done enough or missed something important there. But now my mind goes to finances, decisions at work, things involving staff, deadlines, stuff around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter—almost anything can become the thing I might mess up.\n\nSo when I recheck, it’s usually because I’m afraid I’ve overlooked something, and then even a small mistake starts to feel like it could unravel into a bigger problem. I don’t know if I’d use the word “control” exactly, but… yes, I’m trying to make sure things don’t go wrong, or don’t get worse because I missed something.\n\nIt’s like over the past year my worrying has spread out so much. At first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and whether I was doing enough there, but now it’s finances, decisions at work, staff-related things, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and once my mind latches onto something, even a fairly small thing can start to feel like it’s about to unravel if I don’t keep checking it or managing it closely.\n\nSo I guess the rechecking is my way of trying to keep the outcome from slipping away from me. Like if I check enough, maybe I can prevent the mistake, or prevent the bad consequence, or at least feel like I’ve done everything I could. I know I can’t actually control everything, but in the moment it feels very hard to let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are because you're trying to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s about things being perfect, exactly. Having her in my home changed the whole structure of my life, I guess. It felt like suddenly there was so much more I was responsible for, and the stakes felt higher.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, I’d say that was the turning point. But I wouldn’t say I’ve really thought of it as preventing something bad so much as trying to feel certain enough to move on.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries and repeating actions affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends… I guess I notice it less there than I do at work, but it still affects them indirectly because I’m so preoccupied by whether I’m keeping up and doing things properly.\n\nThe clearest place it’s showing up is at the library. I’ve started missing these small but important details that I normally would catch, and that makes me feel embarrassed and worried. And the grant presentation really shook me—I froze and ended up having to hand off parts of it, which is just not like me. Since then I’ve been worrying a lot about whether I can still lead my team well, whether people are having to compensate for me, that kind of thing.\n\nSo in terms of relationships, I’m not having huge arguments with family or friends over it, but I do feel more distracted and less present because my mind keeps circling around whether I’m letting things slip.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something specific that triggers your need to recheck things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less one single trigger and more that something catches on uncertainty, if that makes sense. Like if there’s a situation where I can’t quite feel sure I did something correctly, or I can’t fully control what might happen, then my mind just grabs onto it.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, honestly. Once a worry starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or whether I missed some little detail at work—I can’t seem to shelve it in my mind. And I keep trying to, because I know how it sounds. I’ll make lists, I’ll go back through what I already did, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you handled this,” and then five minutes later I’m mentally pulling it back off the shelf again and looking at it from another angle.\n\nSo the rechecking usually happens when I get that feeling of, “What if I overlooked something?” It’s not always dramatic. Sometimes it’s just a small doubt, but it doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts to feel urgent, like I need to resolve it before I can move on. And redirecting myself doesn’t always work, even when I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are completely unrelated to any real problem you're facing, but still keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nMost of the thoughts that keep coming back seem tied to something real, even if I know I’m probably making too much of it or turning it over too many times. It’s usually something that could actually matter, or something I feel responsible for. I don’t usually have, like, random strange thoughts out of nowhere that have no connection to my life and then just keep repeating.\n\nI mean, sometimes the connection might be kind of stretched, because I’ll take a small concern and then follow it too far. But it still starts from something real. So… no, I haven’t really noticed thoughts that feel completely unrelated to any actual problem. It’s more that real problems—or possible problems—get stuck in my mind longer than they probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep coming back to you and are not related to any real problem or responsibility you have?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do, no. I mean, I’m trying to think of an example, because I don’t want to answer too quickly and miss what you’re asking, but… the things that repeat in my mind usually have some kind of hook in real life.\n\nLike, they’re connected to something I feel responsible for, or something I think I might have mishandled, or something that could have consequences if I got it wrong. Even if I know I’m probably taking it too far, it doesn’t feel completely random or detached from an actual concern.\n\nSo if you mean thoughts that just come out of nowhere and don’t connect to anything I’m dealing with, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more that ordinary worries get stuck and keep looping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must perform these repeating actions in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it has to be done in a very specific way, no. It’s not like I have a fixed sequence in my head, or that I have to touch something a certain number of times, or do it in exactly the same order every time for it to “count.”\n\nIt’s more… I feel like I need enough reassurance from the action, if that makes sense. Like if I’m rechecking something, I’m trying to get to that feeling of, “Okay, I really did look at it carefully, I didn’t miss anything.” But the way I do it can vary. Sometimes I’ll reread something once, sometimes I’ll go back through it more slowly, sometimes I’ll compare it against a list. It’s not really ritualized in a precise way.\n\nThe anxiety goes down a little if I feel I’ve been thorough, but I don’t think there’s a special method I’m compelled to follow. It’s more about trying to quiet the doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the worries that lead you to recheck things are related to something potentially disastrous happening if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always “disastrous” in a dramatic way, like I’m picturing one single terrible event every time, but it does often feel like if I don’t check, something could start going wrong and then keep getting worse.\n\nOver this past year especially, it’s spread out a lot. At first I think most of my worry was centered on my mother’s safety, like whether something might happen and I wouldn’t catch it or I hadn’t done enough. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, things at work, decisions involving staff, deadlines, the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they’re on the verge of unraveling if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nSo when I recheck, it’s usually because my mind is saying, “If you missed this, there could be consequences.” Maybe not always a catastrophe, but something bad enough that I feel responsible for preventing it. And I know sometimes the level of worry doesn’t match the situation, but in the moment it can feel like I’m keeping something from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are mainly because you're afraid of making a mistake if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not always just, “Oh no, I made a typo,” or something simple like that, but it’s that feeling that if I don’t catch something, I’ll be responsible for what happens next.\n\nAnd over the past year it’s gotten… broader, I guess. It used to be much more focused on my mother’s safety, like worrying I hadn’t done enough or missed something important there. But now my mind goes to finances, decisions at work, things involving staff, deadlines, stuff around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter—almost anything can become the thing I might mess up.\n\nSo when I recheck, it’s usually because I’m afraid I’ve overlooked something, and then even a small mistake starts to feel like it could unravel into a bigger problem. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on ordinary tasks, but in the moment it really feels like I have to make sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to recheck things is because you're trying to control the outcome of a situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s part of it. I don’t know if I’d use the word “control” exactly, but… yes, I’m trying to make sure things don’t go wrong, or don’t get worse because I missed something.\n\nIt’s like over the past year my worrying has spread out so much. At first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and whether I was doing enough there, but now it’s finances, decisions at work, staff-related things, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and once my mind latches onto something, even a fairly small thing can start to feel like it’s about to unravel if I don’t keep checking it or managing it closely.\n\nSo I guess the rechecking is my way of trying to keep the outcome from slipping away from me. Like if I check enough, maybe I can prevent the mistake, or prevent the bad consequence, or at least feel like I’ve done everything I could. I know I can’t actually control everything, but in the moment it feels very hard to let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are because you're trying to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s about things being perfect, exactly. Perfect sounds more like I’m trying to make everything look flawless or meet some very high standard, and that doesn’t feel quite right.\n\nFor me it’s more that I’m trying to feel sure enough that something isn’t wrong, or that I haven’t missed something important. I mean, sometimes I probably do get caught up in tiny details, and from the outside it may look like I’m trying to make it perfect. But internally it feels less like, “This has to be ideal,” and more like, “If I don’t check this again, I might overlook something and then there could be a problem.”\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s a bit of perfectionism mixed in, but that’s not the main feeling I notice. It feels more like needing reassurance that things are okay, not necessarily perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries and repeating actions are in response to a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it started in response to a specific change. It’s been going on for about a year and two months now, and the timing lines up pretty clearly with my mother moving into my home after she had a fall.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I worried about things like anyone does, but this particular pattern—the repeated checking, the feeling that I have to keep making sure I haven’t missed something—really began after that happened. Having her in my home changed the whole structure of my life, I guess. It felt like suddenly there was so much more I was responsible for, and the stakes felt higher.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, I’d say that was the turning point. Since she moved in after the fall, this has just sort of grown and stayed with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42.2, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having a really hard time stopping worries once they start. It’s like something gets into my head and I can’t put it away—sorry, that’s probably a librarian way to say it, but it really feels like I can’t shelve it mentally.\n\nIt can be about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or just whether I missed something important at work. I’ll make lists, I’ll check things, I’ll try to redirect myself to another task, but the worry just keeps circling back. And even when I know I’ve done what I can, I can’t seem to get my mind to accept that and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them rituals, exactly. I do repeat things sometimes, like rechecking a list or looking back at an email, but it feels more like I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t miss something, not like I have a specific rule I have to follow.\n\nI don’t have, you know, a set number of times I do something, or a particular routine that has to be done in a certain order. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by rituals.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any unwanted, intrusive thoughts that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—if by intrusive you mean they sort of barge in and then won’t leave. It’s not usually some bizarre image or anything like that. It’s more like a worry gets a foothold, and then it keeps coming back no matter how many times I try to put it aside.\n\nLike with my mother getting up at night, I’ll start thinking, “What if she falls?” or “What if I should have arranged something differently?” And then even if I’ve already thought through the practical things, my mind keeps circling back to it. Or with work, a grant report or whether I missed something in the collection records—once that thought starts, I can’t seem to shelve it, if that makes sense. I’ll make a list, check it, try to redirect myself to another task, but the worry pops back up again like I didn’t address it at all.\n\nSo yes, they keep coming back, and they feel unwanted, but they’re mostly worries about real things that I just can’t seem to turn off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or mentally repeat certain words or phrases to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that specific way. I mean, I do get stuck thinking things through over and over, but it’s not like I have a word or phrase I have to repeat in my head to make the anxiety go down.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself things like, “You’ve checked this,” or “There’s nothing more to do right now,” but that feels more like trying to reason with myself than a mental ritual. It doesn’t have a special wording, and I don’t feel like I have to say it exactly right or anything. It’s more that I’m trying to calm myself down, and honestly it doesn’t always work very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong urge to perform specific physical actions, like handwashing or checking locks, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get a strong urge in that way, no. Like, I’m not someone who feels driven to wash my hands over and over, or check a lock a certain number of times because I feel something terrible will happen if I don’t.\n\nI do check things sometimes, but it’s usually more ordinary, if that makes sense—like I might look back to make sure I turned something off or that I locked up properly, especially if I’m already keyed up. But I don’t have a specific physical action that I feel compelled to do every time a worry comes up. It’s more that I get caught in the doubt afterward, thinking, “Did I actually do it?” rather than feeling like I have to perform a ritual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions, such as rechecking a list, are done to try and reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair. When I recheck a list or go back over something, I’m usually trying to quiet the worry down—like to prove to myself that I didn’t miss anything. But the frustrating part is that it doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nI came in because once a worry starts—about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or whether I overlooked something at work—I can’t seem to shelve it in my mind. I’ll make a list, then maybe check the list, then try to move on to something else, but it’s like my brain keeps pulling the file back out again. The checking helps for a moment sometimes, but then the doubt comes back and I’m right back in it.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries and repeating actions interfering with your daily life, work, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is starting to interfere, and that’s honestly one of the reasons I’m more worried about it now. It’s not just that I feel tense or preoccupied internally—it’s showing up in my work.\n\nAt the library, I’ve started missing small but important details, the kind of things I normally pride myself on catching. And recently with a grant presentation, I just… froze. I couldn’t get myself back on track the way I usually can, and I had to hand off parts of it to someone else. That was really unsettling because I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and helps the team move forward.\n\nSo then of course I worry about that too—whether I’m still doing a good job leading people, whether my team can rely on me, whether I’m letting things slip. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s enough that I notice it, and it doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when these worries and repeating actions are happening?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. Not usually like a full-blown panic feeling, but it can feel very consuming—like my whole mind narrows down to the one thing I’m worried about, and I can’t get comfortable until I’ve gone back over it again.\n\nWhen it’s happening, I feel tense and kind of trapped in it. I’ll know, logically, that rechecking something probably isn’t going to solve the larger feeling, but in the moment it feels hard not to do it because the uncertainty is so uncomfortable. And then if I do check, I might get a little relief, but I also feel frustrated with myself, like, “Why can’t I just trust that I did it right the first time?”\n\nSo I’d say the distress is moderate to sometimes fairly high, depending on what the worry is about. It’s not constant agony, but it wears me down, and it can make it hard to focus on anything else until the worry loosens its grip a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, it feels a little like that in the moment, but I don’t know that I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more… I’m trying to get rid of the uncertainty, I think.\n\nLike, if I go back over something, it’s usually because I have this nagging feeling that I may have missed something or gotten something wrong, and I want to settle that feeling. I suppose in a broad way that could be about preventing a bad outcome, but it doesn’t always feel that concrete to me. It’s not like there’s one specific disaster I’m picturing every time.\n\nIt’s more that I feel uncomfortable not knowing for sure, and checking gives me a little bit of reassurance—at least briefly. But I wouldn’t say I’ve really thought of it as preventing something bad so much as trying to feel certain enough to move on.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries and repeating actions affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: With family or friends… I guess I notice it less there than I do at work, but it still affects them indirectly because I’m so preoccupied by whether I’m keeping up and doing things properly.\n\nThe clearest place it’s showing up is at the library. I’ve started missing these small but important details that I normally would catch, and that makes me feel embarrassed and worried. And the grant presentation really shook me—I froze and ended up having to hand off parts of it, which is just not like me. Since then I’ve been worrying a lot about whether I can still lead my team well, whether people are having to compensate for me, that kind of thing.\n\nSo in terms of relationships, I’m not having huge arguments with family or friends over it, but I do feel more distracted and less present because my mind keeps circling around whether I’m letting things slip.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something specific that triggers your need to recheck things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less one single trigger and more that something catches on uncertainty, if that makes sense. Like if there’s a situation where I can’t quite feel sure I did something correctly, or I can’t fully control what might happen, then my mind just grabs onto it.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, honestly. Once a worry starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or whether I missed some little detail at work—I can’t seem to shelve it in my mind. And I keep trying to, because I know how it sounds. I’ll make lists, I’ll go back through what I already did, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you handled this,” and then five minutes later I’m mentally pulling it back off the shelf again and looking at it from another angle.\n\nSo the rechecking usually happens when I get that feeling of, “What if I overlooked something?” It’s not always dramatic. Sometimes it’s just a small doubt, but it doesn’t stay small in my head. It starts to feel urgent, like I need to resolve it before I can move on. And redirecting myself doesn’t always work, even when I’m really trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are completely unrelated to any real problem you're facing, but still keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nMost of the thoughts that keep coming back seem tied to something real, even if I know I’m probably making too much of it or turning it over too many times. It’s usually something that could actually matter, or something I feel responsible for. I don’t usually have, like, random strange thoughts out of nowhere that have no connection to my life and then just keep repeating.\n\nI mean, sometimes the connection might be kind of stretched, because I’ll take a small concern and then follow it too far. But it still starts from something real. So… no, I haven’t really noticed thoughts that feel completely unrelated to any actual problem. It’s more that real problems—or possible problems—get stuck in my mind longer than they probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep coming back to you and are not related to any real problem or responsibility you have?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do, no. I mean, I’m trying to think of an example, because I don’t want to answer too quickly and miss what you’re asking, but… the things that repeat in my mind usually have some kind of hook in real life.\n\nLike, they’re connected to something I feel responsible for, or something I think I might have mishandled, or something that could have consequences if I got it wrong. Even if I know I’m probably taking it too far, it doesn’t feel completely random or detached from an actual concern.\n\nSo if you mean thoughts that just come out of nowhere and don’t connect to anything I’m dealing with, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s more that ordinary worries get stuck and keep looping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must perform these repeating actions in a very specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it has to be done in a very specific way, no. It’s not like I have a fixed sequence in my head, or that I have to touch something a certain number of times, or do it in exactly the same order every time for it to “count.”\n\nIt’s more… I feel like I need enough reassurance from the action, if that makes sense. Like if I’m rechecking something, I’m trying to get to that feeling of, “Okay, I really did look at it carefully, I didn’t miss anything.” But the way I do it can vary. Sometimes I’ll reread something once, sometimes I’ll go back through it more slowly, sometimes I’ll compare it against a list. It’s not really ritualized in a precise way.\n\nThe anxiety goes down a little if I feel I’ve been thorough, but I don’t think there’s a special method I’m compelled to follow. It’s more about trying to quiet the doubt.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the worries that lead you to recheck things are related to something potentially disastrous happening if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always “disastrous” in a dramatic way, like I’m picturing one single terrible event every time, but it does often feel like if I don’t check, something could start going wrong and then keep getting worse.\n\nOver this past year especially, it’s spread out a lot. At first I think most of my worry was centered on my mother’s safety, like whether something might happen and I wouldn’t catch it or I hadn’t done enough. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, things at work, decisions involving staff, deadlines, the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter. Even small things can start to feel like they’re on the verge of unraveling if I don’t stay on top of them.\n\nSo when I recheck, it’s usually because my mind is saying, “If you missed this, there could be consequences.” Maybe not always a catastrophe, but something bad enough that I feel responsible for preventing it. And I know sometimes the level of worry doesn’t match the situation, but in the moment it can feel like I’m keeping something from falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are mainly because you're afraid of making a mistake if you don't?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a big part of it. It’s not always just, “Oh no, I made a typo,” or something simple like that, but it’s that feeling that if I don’t catch something, I’ll be responsible for what happens next.\n\nAnd over the past year it’s gotten… broader, I guess. It used to be much more focused on my mother’s safety, like worrying I hadn’t done enough or missed something important there. But now my mind goes to finances, decisions at work, things involving staff, deadlines, stuff around the house, whether I’m being a good daughter—almost anything can become the thing I might mess up.\n\nSo when I recheck, it’s usually because I’m afraid I’ve overlooked something, and then even a small mistake starts to feel like it could unravel into a bigger problem. I know that probably sounds like a lot to put on ordinary tasks, but in the moment it really feels like I have to make sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to recheck things is because you're trying to control the outcome of a situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s part of it. I don’t know if I’d use the word “control” exactly, but… yes, I’m trying to make sure things don’t go wrong, or don’t get worse because I missed something.\n\nIt’s like over the past year my worrying has spread out so much. At first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and whether I was doing enough there, but now it’s finances, decisions at work, staff-related things, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and once my mind latches onto something, even a fairly small thing can start to feel like it’s about to unravel if I don’t keep checking it or managing it closely.\n\nSo I guess the rechecking is my way of trying to keep the outcome from slipping away from me. Like if I check enough, maybe I can prevent the mistake, or prevent the bad consequence, or at least feel like I’ve done everything I could. I know I can’t actually control everything, but in the moment it feels very hard to let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are because you're trying to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s about things being perfect, exactly. Perfect sounds more like I’m trying to make everything look flawless or meet some very high standard, and that doesn’t feel quite right.\n\nFor me it’s more that I’m trying to feel sure enough that something isn’t wrong, or that I haven’t missed something important. I mean, sometimes I probably do get caught up in tiny details, and from the outside it may look like I’m trying to make it perfect. But internally it feels less like, “This has to be ideal,” and more like, “If I don’t check this again, I might overlook something and then there could be a problem.”\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s a bit of perfectionism mixed in, but that’s not the main feeling I notice. It feels more like needing reassurance that things are okay, not necessarily perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries and repeating actions are in response to a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it started in response to a specific change. It’s been going on for about a year and two months now, and the timing lines up pretty clearly with my mother moving into my home after she had a fall.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I worried about things like anyone does, but this particular pattern—the repeated checking, the feeling that I have to keep making sure I haven’t missed something—really began after that happened. Having her in my home changed the whole structure of my life, I guess. It felt like suddenly there was so much more I was responsible for, and the stakes felt higher.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, I’d say that was the turning point. Since she moved in after the fall, this has just sort of grown and stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and repeating actions have been present for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely more than six months. It’s been about a year and two months now, as best as I can place it.\n\nIt really began around when my mother moved into my home after her fall. That’s the point I keep coming back to, because before then I don’t remember this being such a constant thing. Since then it’s just sort of stayed with me, and I suppose it’s become part of the rhythm of my days in a way I didn’t expect. So yes… more than six months, for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know if I’d use the word “control” exactly, but… yes, I’m trying to make sure things don’t go wrong, or don’t get worse because I missed something.\n\nIt’s like over the past year my worrying has spread out so much. At first it was mostly about my mother’s safety, and whether I was doing enough there, but now it’s finances, decisions at work, staff-related things, deadlines, the house, whether I’m being a good daughter… and once my mind latches onto something, even a fairly small thing can start to feel like it’s about to unravel if I don’t keep checking it or managing it closely.\n\nSo I guess the rechecking is my way of trying to keep the outcome from slipping away from me. Like if I check enough, maybe I can prevent the mistake, or prevent the bad consequence, or at least feel like I’ve done everything I could. I know I can’t actually control everything, but in the moment it feels very hard to let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeating actions are because you're trying to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s about things being perfect, exactly. Perfect sounds more like I’m trying to make everything look flawless or meet some very high standard, and that doesn’t feel quite right.\n\nFor me it’s more that I’m trying to feel sure enough that something isn’t wrong, or that I haven’t missed something important. I mean, sometimes I probably do get caught up in tiny details, and from the outside it may look like I’m trying to make it perfect. But internally it feels less like, “This has to be ideal,” and more like, “If I don’t check this again, I might overlook something and then there could be a problem.”\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s a bit of perfectionism mixed in, but that’s not the main feeling I notice. It feels more like needing reassurance that things are okay, not necessarily perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries and repeating actions are in response to a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it started in response to a specific change. It’s been going on for about a year and two months now, and the timing lines up pretty clearly with my mother moving into my home after she had a fall.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I worried about things like anyone does, but this particular pattern—the repeated checking, the feeling that I have to keep making sure I haven’t missed something—really began after that happened. Having her in my home changed the whole structure of my life, I guess. It felt like suddenly there was so much more I was responsible for, and the stakes felt higher.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, I’d say that was the turning point. Since she moved in after the fall, this has just sort of grown and stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries and repeating actions have been present for more than six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely more than six months. It’s been about a year and two months now, as best as I can place it.\n\nIt really began around when my mother moved into my home after her fall. That’s the point I keep coming back to, because before then I don’t remember this being such a constant thing. Since then it’s just sort of stayed with me, and I suppose it’s become part of the rhythm of my days in a way I didn’t expect. So yes… more than six months, for sure."
